From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I came in ’cause I just can’t sit still. It’s like I got a motor running inside me all day, you know? Like even if I’m at church, or sitting here in a clinic, or just at home, I start feeling trapped in my own skin and I gotta get up, move around, do something.\n\nIt’s annoying too, ’cause folks act like I’m being rude, but I’m not trying to be. I remember at church the other week—well, not even just that week, it happens a lot—I’m sitting there and everybody else is calm, and I’m tapping or shifting, and then I’m thinking about the pews being too hard, and then I’m looking at the door like I need air. So… yeah, that’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus on one thing for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, yeah, I do. Like if somebody’s telling me something for a while—like ride times, or what we need from the pantry, or plans for church—I’m listening at first, I really am. But then my mind just kinda… slides off somewhere else, and next thing I know I missed the part I actually needed.\n\nAnd then I feel dumb asking again, ’cause they’ll be like, “I just told you.” It’s not that I don’t care. It just don’t stay put in my head sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble sitting still or paying attention when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I did. School was… whew, yeah. Teachers were always moving my seat around, like putting me up front or away from certain kids, ’cause I couldn’t stay in my chair. I’d be talking when I wasn’t supposed to, blurting stuff out.\n\nAnd I remember I used to jump up before class was even done—like I already had my books or I was halfway standing, and the teacher would get on me for it. I don’t know, I just felt like once I was ready to go, I had to go. Then I’d get in trouble, and my mama would hear about it, and… yeah, it was a whole thing back then too.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're talking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do that a lot. Like somebody’ll still be talking and I already got the answer jumping out my mouth.\n\nLike the pastor might be asking something, or my cousin’s trying to explain what he means, or the lady at the pantry’s still telling me the thing, and I’ll just cut in with an answer before they’re done. Then halfway after I say it, I realize, oh… that wasn’t even what they were asking. Or I see their face and I can tell I made ’em mad.\n\nI don’t mean to be disrespectful. It just comes out fast, like I gotta say it right then before it disappears.\n\nDoctor: Do you often do things on impulse, like acting without thinking?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I do, mostly with my mouth, if that counts. Like I’ll jump in before somebody’s even finished talking—pastor, my cousin, the lady at the pantry, whoever—and I’ll be answering what I think they’re asking.\n\nThen a second later I’m like, wait… that wasn’t even it. Or I can see on their face they’re annoyed, like I cut them off. I don’t be trying to be rude, it just comes out before I really think it through.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse in physical ways, like doing reckless things without thinking?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… reckless like what exactly? I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m out here doing wild stuff on purpose.\n\nSometimes I move before I think, I guess, but when you say physical reckless things, my mind goes to like… jumping off roofs or driving crazy or something. I don’t really know if that’s me. I mostly notice it when I’m talking, like I already said.\n\nBut physical stuff… hmm. I’d have to think on that. I don’t got a clear example popping up right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you often do physical things without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’m still not real sure what counts as “physical things.” Like I don’t really have something in my head where I’m like, yeah, I do that all the time.\n\nI might get up and move around without thinking, but that’s not like… reckless. And see, my uncle used to always say “think before you move,” but he meant it about everything, like even fixing a sink, and then he’d talk for an hour about tools—sorry, I’m getting off track.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed myself doing physical stuff without thinking it through, not in a clear way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make impulsive decisions when you're alone, like when you're shopping or doing chores?\n\nPatient: Hmm… when I’m alone? Like shopping, I don’t know. I don’t really go thinking, “oh I’m being impulsive right now.” I might pick something up and then put it back, but that’s just shopping, right?\n\nChores… I’ll start doing one thing and then notice something else, like if I’m sweeping and then I see the trash needs going out, and then I’m thinking about how the trash pickup used to come real early on my old street—like before the sun was even up—and my neighbor would get mad if the bins were too close to his driveway…\n\nBut, uh… impulsive decisions alone, I can’t really say. Nothing clear comes to mind like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're doing quiet activities at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, that one I do notice. Like if I’m supposed to be doing something quiet at home, just sitting there, I feel like there’s a motor running inside me. Like my body’s saying get up, get up, get up.\n\nIt’s not even just home, really. Church is bad for that too, and sitting here in the clinic sometimes, I start feeling trapped, like I need to move or do something with my hands or stand up. At home I’ll try to sit and watch something, but then I’m up messing with something, walking around, checking stuff. I don’t know, it’s like sitting still makes me feel boxed in.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble sitting still and paying attention, even as an adult?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, even back when I was a boy in school, they were always moving my seat around. Like, “sit up front,” “sit by the teacher,” “sit away from so-and-so,” because I couldn’t really stay put.\n\nI’d talk when I wasn’t supposed to, jump up before class was even done… and then I’d get in trouble for it. I remember one teacher put me near the pencil sharpener, which was a bad idea because then every little thing—well, anyway. Yeah, it’s been like that since I was young.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had problems at work or with relationships because of your difficulty sitting still or paying attention?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, that’s been a problem. Not like a regular job right now, but with people depending on me, like at church or the pantry, or family stuff.\n\nThey’ll ask me to give somebody a ride, or remember an appointment, or bring supplies, and I really mean to do it. I’m not trying to be careless. But then something gets mixed up or I lose track, or I’m moving around doing ten things and the main thing slips. Then people stop asking me. Or they ask somebody else because they don’t trust I’ll come through.\n\nAnd with family… that hurts more, because they’ll say, “never mind, we’ll handle it,” and I know why they’re saying it. I feel like I keep letting everybody down even when I was trying to help.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your inability to sit still or pay attention has caused you a lot of stress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a lot of stress. It kind of follows me around.\n\nLike at church, or over at the pantry, people will ask me to do something simple—give somebody a ride, remember an appointment, bring supplies—and I say yes because I want to help. I really do. Then I get turned around, or I’m rushing around with other stuff, and somehow I don’t come through right.\n\nAnd then after a while people stop asking me. Or they ask someone else right in front of me, and I know why. Same with my family… they’ll say, “Never mind, we got it,” and that just sits on me. Makes me feel like I keep letting everybody down, even when I meant good.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these feelings of restlessness and difficulty paying attention in most areas of your life, such as at work, at home, and in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s… it’s kind of everywhere, not just one place.\n\nLike at church and the pantry, people used to ask me to help with rides or bringing supplies, or remembering when somebody had to be somewhere, and I’d say yes. Then I’d get mixed up or distracted, and now they don’t really trust me with that stuff.\n\nAnd at home too, with my family, same kind of thing. They’ll just say, “We’ll handle it,” and I know what that means. I mean, I’m trying to help, I really am, but it ends up looking like I don’t care. It makes me feel like I’m letting everybody down.\n\nDoctor: Have you had difficulty maintaining friendships or romantic relationships because of your restlessness or difficulty paying attention?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, friendships and family, mostly. Like if somebody’s telling me something for a while—like ride times, or what we need from the pantry, or plans for church—I’m listening at first, I really am. But then my mind just kinda… slides off somewhere else, and next thing I know I missed the part I actually needed.\n\nAnd then I feel dumb asking again, ’cause they’ll be like, “I just told you.” It’s not that I don’t care. It just don’t stay put in my head sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble sitting still or paying attention when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I did. School was… whew, yeah. Teachers were always moving my seat around, like putting me up front or away from certain kids, ’cause I couldn’t stay in my chair. I’d be talking when I wasn’t supposed to, blurting stuff out.\n\nAnd I remember I used to jump up before class was even done—like I already had my books or I was halfway standing, and the teacher would get on me for it. I don’t know, I just felt like once I was ready to go, I had to go. Then I’d get in trouble, and my mama would hear about it, and… yeah, it was a whole thing back then too.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're talking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do that a lot. Like somebody’ll still be talking and I already got the answer jumping out my mouth.\n\nLike the pastor might be asking something, or my cousin’s trying to explain what he means, or the lady at the pantry’s still telling me the thing, and I’ll just cut in with an answer before they’re done. Then halfway after I say it, I realize, oh… that wasn’t even what they were asking. Or I see their face and I can tell I made ’em mad.\n\nI don’t mean to be disrespectful. It just comes out fast, like I gotta say it right then before it disappears.\n\nDoctor: Do you often do things on impulse, like acting without thinking?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I do, mostly with my mouth, if that counts. Like I’ll jump in before somebody’s even finished talking—pastor, my cousin, the lady at the pantry, whoever—and I’ll be answering what I think they’re asking.\n\nThen a second later I’m like, wait… that wasn’t even it. Or I can see on their face they’re annoyed, like I cut them off. I don’t be trying to be rude, it just comes out before I really think it through.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse in physical ways, like doing reckless things without thinking?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… reckless like what exactly? I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m out here doing wild stuff on purpose.\n\nSometimes I move before I think, I guess, but when you say physical reckless things, my mind goes to like… jumping off roofs or driving crazy or something. I don’t really know if that’s me. I mostly notice it when I’m talking, like I already said.\n\nBut physical stuff… hmm. I’d have to think on that. I don’t got a clear example popping up right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you often do physical things without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’m still not real sure what counts as “physical things.” Like I don’t really have something in my head where I’m like, yeah, I do that all the time.\n\nI might get up and move around without thinking, but that’s not like… reckless. And see, my uncle used to always say “think before you move,” but he meant it about everything, like even fixing a sink, and then he’d talk for an hour about tools—sorry, I’m getting off track.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed myself doing physical stuff without thinking it through, not in a clear way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make impulsive decisions when you're alone, like when you're shopping or doing chores?\n\nPatient: Hmm… when I’m alone? Like shopping, I don’t know. I don’t really go thinking, “oh I’m being impulsive right now.” I might pick something up and then put it back, but that’s just shopping, right?\n\nChores… I’ll start doing one thing and then notice something else, like if I’m sweeping and then I see the trash needs going out, and then I’m thinking about how the trash pickup used to come real early on my old street—like before the sun was even up—and my neighbor would get mad if the bins were too close to his driveway…\n\nBut, uh… impulsive decisions alone, I can’t really say. Nothing clear comes to mind like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're doing quiet activities at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, that one I do notice. Like if I’m supposed to be doing something quiet at home, just sitting there, I feel like there’s a motor running inside me. Like my body’s saying get up, get up, get up.\n\nIt’s not even just home, really. Church is bad for that too, and sitting here in the clinic sometimes, I start feeling trapped, like I need to move or do something with my hands or stand up. At home I’ll try to sit and watch something, but then I’m up messing with something, walking around, checking stuff. I don’t know, it’s like sitting still makes me feel boxed in.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble sitting still and paying attention, even as an adult?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, even back when I was a boy in school, they were always moving my seat around. Like, “sit up front,” “sit by the teacher,” “sit away from so-and-so,” because I couldn’t really stay put.\n\nI’d talk when I wasn’t supposed to, jump up before class was even done… and then I’d get in trouble for it. I remember one teacher put me near the pencil sharpener, which was a bad idea because then every little thing—well, anyway. Yeah, it’s been like that since I was young.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had problems at work or with relationships because of your difficulty sitting still or paying attention?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, that’s been a problem. Not like a regular job right now, but with people depending on me, like at church or the pantry, or family stuff.\n\nThey’ll ask me to give somebody a ride, or remember an appointment, or bring supplies, and I really mean to do it. I’m not trying to be careless. But then something gets mixed up or I lose track, or I’m moving around doing ten things and the main thing slips. Then people stop asking me. Or they ask somebody else because they don’t trust I’ll come through.\n\nAnd with family… that hurts more, because they’ll say, “never mind, we’ll handle it,” and I know why they’re saying it. I feel like I keep letting everybody down even when I was trying to help.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your inability to sit still or pay attention has caused you a lot of stress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a lot of stress. It kind of follows me around.\n\nLike at church, or over at the pantry, people will ask me to do something simple—give somebody a ride, remember an appointment, bring supplies—and I say yes because I want to help. I really do. Then I get turned around, or I’m rushing around with other stuff, and somehow I don’t come through right.\n\nAnd then after a while people stop asking me. Or they ask someone else right in front of me, and I know why. Same with my family… they’ll say, “Never mind, we got it,” and that just sits on me. Makes me feel like I keep letting everybody down, even when I meant good.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these feelings of restlessness and difficulty paying attention in most areas of your life, such as at work, at home, and in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s… it’s kind of everywhere, not just one place.\n\nLike at church and the pantry, people used to ask me to help with rides or bringing supplies, or remembering when somebody had to be somewhere, and I’d say yes. Then I’d get mixed up or distracted, and now they don’t really trust me with that stuff.\n\nAnd at home too, with my family, same kind of thing. They’ll just say, “We’ll handle it,” and I know what that means. I mean, I’m trying to help, I really am, but it ends up looking like I don’t care. It makes me feel like I’m letting everybody down.\n\nDoctor: Have you had difficulty maintaining friendships or romantic relationships because of your restlessness or difficulty paying attention?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, friendships and family, mostly. Romantic stuff, I don’t know, that’s its own whole mess, but with people close to me, yeah.\n\nLike at church or the pantry, they used to count on me more. “Can you give so-and-so a ride?” or “Don’t forget this appointment,” or “Bring these supplies.” And I’d say yes, because I wanted to help. Then I’d get busy, or turned around, or I’d be doing one thing and forget the thing I promised.\n\nAfter a while people just… they stop asking. Or they ask somebody else, and they don’t say it mean, but I can tell. Same with my family. They’ll be like, “Never mind, we’ll handle it,” and that hurts. Makes me feel like I keep letting everybody down even when I’m trying to do right.\n\nDoctor: Have you often felt like you're disturbing others or making it hard for them to focus when you're with them in quiet settings?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I think so. I don’t mean to, you know, I’m trying to sit there and be respectful, especially if it’s quiet, but my body just keeps doing stuff.\n\nLike my leg’ll be bouncing without me even noticing, or my fingers are tapping on the chair, and then I catch somebody looking over. Or I’ll have something in my hands and I’m twisting it around the whole time. I try to stop, but then I’m thinking about stopping, and—well, then I start up again somehow. It’s embarrassing, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so focused on something that you lose track of everything else around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I don’t think so? Not like where everything disappears around me.\n\nSometimes I’ll get caught up in something for a bit, but then some other thought comes in, or I remember something else, or somebody says something and I’m off on that. Like, I don’t know if that’s what you mean by focused. My brother can sit with one thing forever—he used to do that with fixing stuff, like radios and little motors, and I’d be standing there handing him tools and then I’d wander off because I remembered the trash or… anyway.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t really noticed losing track of everything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever become so intensely focused on an activity that you tune out everything else around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t really think so… I mean, maybe I get interested in something for a minute, but “tune out everything else,” I don’t know. That sounds more like when somebody’s watching a game and you can wave your hand in front of them and they don’t hear you.\n\nWith me it’s usually the opposite, I guess. I’ll be trying to do one thing and then I hear a door close or remember I left something somewhere, and then I’m off thinking about that. But, uh… no, I can’t really think of times where I’m so locked in that the whole world goes away.\n\nDoctor: Have you had these problems with restlessness and paying attention your whole life, or did they start at some point as an adult?\n\nPatient: No, that’s been… that’s been since I was a kid, really. I remember in school, teachers kept moving my seat around because I couldn’t just stay put. I’d be talking when I wasn’t supposed to, or I’d jump up before class was even over.\n\nThey used to put me closer to the teacher, then in the back, then away from certain kids… like it was gonna fix it. I don’t know. I got in trouble a lot for that stuff when I was a boy. So it didn’t just start when I was grown.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life where these problems with restlessness and paying attention went away or got much better?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, it’s always kind of been there, like I said, but it’s been really hard to manage for… I guess about two years and a couple months now.\n\nThat was around when my older relative’s health changed, and I had to start taking on more stuff. Appointments, errands, keeping track of things—well, trying to keep track. And then it all just felt a lot harder. Before that it wasn’t gone, but I could sort of get by better, maybe because I didn’t have as much on me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F90.0", "F90.8", "F90.9"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

Yeah… yeah, it has. I mean, I’m not working right now, but even with regular stuff, people don’t really trust me with things like they used to.

I don’t know, it’s like sitting still makes me feel boxed in.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble sitting still and paying attention, even as an adult?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, even back when I was a boy in school, they were always moving my seat around. Like, “sit up front,” “sit by the teacher,” “sit away from so-and-so,” because I couldn’t really stay put.\n\nI’d talk when I wasn’t supposed to, jump up before class was even done… and then I’d get in trouble for it. I remember one teacher put me near the pencil sharpener, which was a bad idea because then every little thing—well, anyway. Makes me feel like I keep letting everybody down, even when I meant good.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these feelings of restlessness and difficulty paying attention in most areas of your life, such as at work, at home, and in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s… it’s kind of everywhere, not just one place.\n\nLike at church and the pantry, people used to ask me to help with rides or bringing supplies, or remembering when somebody had to be somewhere, and I’d say yes. They’ll just say, “We’ll handle it,” and I know what that means. I mean, I’m trying to help, I really am, but it ends up looking like I don’t care. It makes me feel like I’m letting everybody down.\n\nDoctor: Have you had difficulty maintaining friendships or romantic relationships because of your restlessness or difficulty paying attention?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, friendships and family, mostly. Romantic stuff, I don’t know, that’s its own whole mess, but with people close to me, yeah.\n\nLike at church or the pantry, they used to count on me more. “Can you give so-and-so a ride?” or “Don’t forget this appointment,” or “Bring these supplies.” And I’d say yes, because I wanted to help. It just don’t stay put in my head sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble sitting still or paying attention when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I did. School was… whew, yeah. Teachers were always moving my seat around, like putting me up front or away from certain kids, ’cause I couldn’t stay in my chair. I’d be talking when I wasn’t supposed to, blurting stuff out.\n\nAnd I remember I used to jump up before class was even done—like I already had my books or I was halfway standing, and the teacher would get on me for it. I don’t know, I just felt like once I was ready to go, I had to go. Then I’d get in trouble, and my mama would hear about it, and… yeah, it was a whole thing back then too.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're talking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do that a lot. Like somebody’ll still be talking and I already got the answer jumping out my mouth.\n\nLike the pastor might be asking something, or my cousin’s trying to explain what he means, or the lady at the pantry’s still telling me the thing, and I’ll just cut in with an answer before they’re done. Then halfway after I say it, I realize, oh… that wasn’t even what they were asking. Or I see their face and I can tell I made ’em mad.\n\nI don’t mean to be disrespectful. It just comes out fast, like I gotta say it right then before it disappears.\n\nDoctor: Do you often do things on impulse, like acting without thinking?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I do, mostly with my mouth, if that counts. Like I’ll jump in before somebody’s even finished talking—pastor, my cousin, the lady at the pantry, whoever—and I’ll be answering what I think they’re asking.\n\nThen a second later I’m like, wait… that wasn’t even it. Or I can see on their face they’re annoyed, like I cut them off. I don’t be trying to be rude, it just comes out before I really think it through.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse in physical ways, like doing reckless things without thinking?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… reckless like what exactly? I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m out here doing wild stuff on purpose.\n\nSometimes I move before I think, I guess, but when you say physical reckless things, my mind goes to like… jumping off roofs or driving crazy or something. I don’t really know if that’s me. I mostly notice it when I’m talking, like I already said.\n\nBut physical stuff… hmm. I’d have to think on that. I don’t got a clear example popping up right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you often do physical things without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’m still not real sure what counts as “physical things.” Like I don’t really have something in my head where I’m like, yeah, I do that all the time.\n\nI might get up and move around without thinking, but that’s not like… reckless. And see, my uncle used to always say “think before you move,” but he meant it about everything, like even fixing a sink, and then he’d talk for an hour about tools—sorry, I’m getting off track.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed myself doing physical stuff without thinking it through, not in a clear way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make impulsive decisions when you're alone, like when you're shopping or doing chores?\n\nPatient: Hmm… when I’m alone? Like shopping, I don’t know. I don’t really go thinking, “oh I’m being impulsive right now.” I might pick something up and then put it back, but that’s just shopping, right?\n\nChores… I’ll start doing one thing and then notice something else, like if I’m sweeping and then I see the trash needs going out, and then I’m thinking about how the trash pickup used to come real early on my old street—like before the sun was even up—and my neighbor would get mad if the bins were too close to his driveway…\n\nBut, uh… impulsive decisions alone, I can’t really say. Nothing clear comes to mind like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're doing quiet activities at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, that one I do notice. Like if I’m supposed to be doing something quiet at home, just sitting there, I feel like there’s a motor running inside me. Like my body’s saying get up, get up, get up.\n\nIt’s not even just home, really. Church is bad for that too, and sitting here in the clinic sometimes, I start feeling trapped, like I need to move or do something with my hands or stand up. At home I’ll try to sit and watch something, but then I’m up messing with something, walking around, checking stuff. I don’t know, it’s like sitting still makes me feel boxed in.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble sitting still and paying attention, even as an adult?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, even back when I was a boy in school, they were always moving my seat around. Like, “sit up front,” “sit by the teacher,” “sit away from so-and-so,” because I couldn’t really stay put.\n\nI’d talk when I wasn’t supposed to, jump up before class was even done… and then I’d get in trouble for it. I remember one teacher put me near the pencil sharpener, which was a bad idea because then every little thing—well, anyway. Yeah, it’s been like that since I was young.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had problems at work or with relationships because of your difficulty sitting still or paying attention?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, that’s been a problem. Not like a regular job right now, but with people depending on me, like at church or the pantry, or family stuff.\n\nThey’ll ask me to give somebody a ride, or remember an appointment, or bring supplies, and I really mean to do it. I’m not trying to be careless. But then something gets mixed up or I lose track, or I’m moving around doing ten things and the main thing slips. Then people stop asking me. Or they ask somebody else because they don’t trust I’ll come through.\n\nAnd with family… that hurts more, because they’ll say, “never mind, we’ll handle it,” and I know why they’re saying it. I feel like I keep letting everybody down even when I was trying to help.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your inability to sit still or pay attention has caused you a lot of stress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a lot of stress. It kind of follows me around.\n\nLike at church, or over at the pantry, people will ask me to do something simple—give somebody a ride, remember an appointment, bring supplies—and I say yes because I want to help. I really do. Then I get turned around, or I’m rushing around with other stuff, and somehow I don’t come through right.\n\nAnd then after a while people stop asking me. Or they ask someone else right in front of me, and I know why. Same with my family… they’ll say, “Never mind, we got it,” and that just sits on me. Makes me feel like I keep letting everybody down, even when I meant good.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these feelings of restlessness and difficulty paying attention in most areas of your life, such as at work, at home, and in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s… it’s kind of everywhere, not just one place.\n\nLike at church and the pantry, people used to ask me to help with rides or bringing supplies, or remembering when somebody had to be somewhere, and I’d say yes. Then I’d get mixed up or distracted, and now they don’t really trust me with that stuff.\n\nAnd at home too, with my family, same kind of thing. They’ll just say, “We’ll handle it,” and I know what that means. I mean, I’m trying to help, I really am, but it ends up looking like I don’t care. It makes me feel like I’m letting everybody down.\n\nDoctor: Have you had difficulty maintaining friendships or romantic relationships because of your restlessness or difficulty paying attention?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, friendships and family, mostly. Romantic stuff, I don’t know, that’s its own whole mess, but with people close to me, yeah.\n\nLike at church or the pantry, they used to count on me more. “Can you give so-and-so a ride?” or “Don’t forget this appointment,” or “Bring these supplies.” And I’d say yes, because I wanted to help. Then I’d get busy, or turned around, or I’d be doing one thing and forget the thing I promised.\n\nAfter a while people just… they stop asking. Or they ask somebody else, and they don’t say it mean, but I can tell. Same with my family. They’ll be like, “Never mind, we’ll handle it,” and that hurts. Makes me feel like I keep letting everybody down even when I’m trying to do right.\n\nDoctor: Have you often felt like you're disturbing others or making it hard for them to focus when you're with them in quiet settings?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I think so. I don’t mean to, you know, I’m trying to sit there and be respectful, especially if it’s quiet, but my body just keeps doing stuff.\n\nLike my leg’ll be bouncing without me even noticing, or my fingers are tapping on the chair, and then I catch somebody looking over. Or I’ll have something in my hands and I’m twisting it around the whole time. I try to stop, but then I’m thinking about stopping, and—well, then I start up again somehow. It’s embarrassing, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so focused on something that you lose track of everything else around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I don’t think so? Not like where everything disappears around me.\n\nSometimes I’ll get caught up in something for a bit, but then some other thought comes in, or I remember something else, or somebody says something and I’m off on that. Like, I don’t know if that’s what you mean by focused. My brother can sit with one thing forever—he used to do that with fixing stuff, like radios and little motors, and I’d be standing there handing him tools and then I’d wander off because I remembered the trash or… anyway.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t really noticed losing track of everything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever become so intensely focused on an activity that you tune out everything else around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t really think so… I mean, maybe I get interested in something for a minute, but “tune out everything else,” I don’t know. That sounds more like when somebody’s watching a game and you can wave your hand in front of them and they don’t hear you.\n\nWith me it’s usually the opposite, I guess. I’ll be trying to do one thing and then I hear a door close or remember I left something somewhere, and then I’m off thinking about that. But, uh… no, I can’t really think of times where I’m so locked in that the whole world goes away.\n\nDoctor: Have you had these problems with restlessness and paying attention your whole life, or did they start at some point as an adult?\n\nPatient: No, that’s been… that’s been since I was a kid, really. I remember in school, teachers kept moving my seat around because I couldn’t just stay put. I’d be talking when I wasn’t supposed to, or I’d jump up before class was even over.\n\nThey used to put me closer to the teacher, then in the back, then away from certain kids… like it was gonna fix it. I don’t know. I got in trouble a lot for that stuff when I was a boy. So it didn’t just start when I was grown.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life where these problems with restlessness and paying attention went away or got much better?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, it’s always kind of been there, like I said, but it’s been really hard to manage for… I guess about two years and a couple months now.\n\nThat was around when my older relative’s health changed, and I had to start taking on more stuff. Appointments, errands, keeping track of things—well, trying to keep track. And then it all just felt a lot harder. Before that it wasn’t gone, but I could sort of get by better, maybe because I didn’t have as much on me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your symptoms have significantly interfered with your ability to work or take care of daily responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, it has. I mean, I’m not working right now, but even with regular stuff, people don’t really trust me with things like they used to.\n\nLike at church, or the food pantry, or even in my own family—if somebody needs a ride, or there’s an appointment to remember, or supplies to bring somewhere, they’ll kind of… not ask me anymore. Or they’ll ask somebody else to double-check me. And I get it, because I’ve messed it up before, but it feels bad.\n\nI’ll mean to help, you know? I really do. Then somehow I’m late, or I forget a piece of it, or I don’t follow through right, and then everybody’s looking at me like, “Why did we count on you?” It makes me feel like I’m letting people down all the time."
